Security (1)

mediumConfirmedverifier suggests: low release effort: small

testflight.sh passes the keychain password on the command line and never re-locks the keychain

scripts/testflight.sh:31 · reviewer: build-release-config
What it is
The script unlocks the shared tvhome-signing keychain with `security unlock-keychain -p <password>`. The password becomes a process argument, so any local process can read it from `ps` while `security` runs. The script has no `trap` and no matching `security lock-keychain`, so the keychain holding the distribution certificate stays unlocked after the script exits, whether it succeeded or failed partway through the archive, export or upload.
Why it matters
An unlocked signing keychain lets any process running as the user sign with the distribution identity without a prompt until the keychain's own auto-lock timeout, if it has one, fires. The keychain is shared with another project (tvhome), so the exposure is wider than this app.
How to fix
Pipe the password to `security unlock-keychain` on stdin instead of using `-p` (when `-p` is omitted, `security` reads the password from stdin). Add `trap 'security lock-keychain "$keychain"' EXIT` right after unlocking so the keychain is re-locked on every exit path, including failures under `set -e`.
Expected improvement
The password no longer shows up in the process table, and the signing keychain is locked again as soon as the release script exits.
Evidence
security unlock-keychain -p "$(env_value SIGNING_KEYCHAIN_PASSWORD)" "$keychain"
Verifier notes (2)
  • CONFIRM (existence lens, high confidence): I read all of /Users/daniel/Projects/pitstop/scripts/testflight.sh (46 lines). Line 31 is exactly `security unlock-keychain -p "$(env_value SIGNING_KEYCHAIN_PASSWORD)" "$keychain"`. The command substitution expands before exec, so the password sits in the argv of `security` and any process can read it with `ps` while that command runs. The script has no `trap` and no `security lock-keychain` anywhere, and it runs under `set -euo pipefail`. So the keychain at $HOME/Library/Keychains/tvhome-signing.keychain-db stays unlocked after every exit path: the `--no-upload` early exit at line 43, the end of a successful upload, and any failure in archive, export or upload. The line number and the evidence are both accurate. Why I suggest low rather than medium: this is a single-user personal Mac. The `ps` window only lasts as long as the brief `security` call. Anything that could exploit either problem would already be running code as the user, and such code could also read the plaintext password straight out of .env. The keychain's own auto-lock settings were not checked. Leaving it unlocked is the bigger of the two issues. A caveat on the fix: when `-p` is omitted, `security unlock-keychain` prompts for the password, and that prompt may read from the terminal (/dev/tty) rather than stdin. Piping the password in is therefore not guaranteed to work and should be tested first. The `trap 'security lock-keychain "$keychain"' EXIT` part is sound. One small catch: because the keychain is shared, re-locking it could interrupt a tvhome signing run happening at the same moment. That is unlikely, and it is not a reason to skip the trap.
  • CONFIRM (impact lens, high confidence): I read /Users/daniel/Projects/pitstop/scripts/testflight.sh and .gitignore, ran `security show-keychain-info` on the keychain, and checked the permissions on .env. Both facts in the finding hold. Line 31 is `security unlock-keychain -p "$(env_value SIGNING_KEYCHAIN_PASSWORD)" "$keychain"`. The script has no trap and no `security lock-keychain` on any exit path. The keychain also has no auto-lock: show-keychain-info reports "no-timeout", so it stays unlocked until logout or reboot, which is worse than the finding's "if it has one". The severity is overstated. The attacker in both halves of the finding is a process running as the same user. On macOS, reading another process's arguments (KERN_PROCARGS2) is limited to that process's owner or root. That same-user process can just read the password from .env (mode 0600, owned by the user) and unlock the keychain itself. So the ps exposure and the lingering unlock add very little beyond the plaintext .env the project already relies on by design ("Secrets live in a gitignored .env ... read by scripts/testflight.sh"). Also, the script header (lines 6-7) says the unlock is deliberate, so signing never stops on a password prompt. Leaving the keychain unlocked is real but minor hygiene on a single-developer machine, so I suggest low, not medium. On the fix: adding `trap 'security lock-keychain "$keychain"' EXIT` right after the unlock is correct and cheap. The stdin suggestion is uncertain. Without -p, `security` prompts for the password, likely through the terminal rather than plain stdin, so piping the password in may not work and should be tested before anyone relies on it. Since the password already sits in .env, the trap is the part worth doing.

Correctness bugs (27)

highConfirmedverifier suggests: medium map effort: small

MapTabView.onAppear re-fits the camera on every return from the List tab, overwriting the saved position

Pitstop/Pitstop/MapTabView.swift:71-73, 93-100 · reviewer: map-clustering
What it is
ContentView switches tabs with a `switch selectedView` inside a Group, so MapTabView is torn down and rebuilt on each List-to-Map switch, and `.onAppear` runs again every time. `onAppear` calls `fitCameraToNearbyBathrooms()` without checking anything first. That function writes `cameraPosition = .region(...)` fitted to the bathrooms, and `apply` then calls `setUserTrackingMode(.none)`. The coordinator works to keep the binding current (ClusteredMapView.swift 162-177: "Keeps the binding current so the map comes back where the user left it after the List tab, still following the user if it was"), but this onAppear replaces that saved position each time. On a cold launch the same call runs before the first location fix arrives. `nearbyBathrooms` then returns every filtered bathroom, so the camera fits pins in other cities, and it also switches off the initial `.userLocation` follow mode set in ContentView.
Why it matters
Once the user has saved any bathrooms, switching to List and back throws away their pan and zoom, and their follow-me mode, and jumps the map to a bathroom bounding box. On launch the map may frame bathrooms hundreds of kilometers away rather than the user's position. That defeats the 50 km nearby rule the comment on `nearbyBathrooms` describes.
How to fix
Fit the camera at most once per app session, or only when the bathroom list goes between empty and non-empty (the onChange already handles that case). For example, keep a `hasFittedCamera` flag in ContentView next to `mapCameraPosition` and skip the fit when the flag is set. Also skip the fit when the binding is still `.userLocation` and location permission is granted, so follow mode survives launch. If a nearby fit is wanted, run it after the first location fix arrives, not in onAppear.
Expected improvement
The map returns exactly where the user left it after the List tab, as the coordinator comment promises, and launch keeps following the user instead of framing distant bathrooms.
Evidence
.onAppear {
            fitCameraToNearbyBathrooms()
        }
...
        withAnimation(.easeInOut(duration: 0.6)) {
            cameraPosition = .region(region)
        }
Verifier notes (2)
  • CONFIRM (existence lens, high confidence): I read Pitstop/Pitstop/MapTabView.swift (lines 1-140), ContentView.swift (lines 30-70) and ClusteredMapView.swift (lines 115-200). The finding holds. What the code does: - ContentView.swift 51-64 switches tabs with `switch selectedView` inside a `Group`. Each List-to-Map switch builds a new MapTabView, so `.onAppear` runs again. - MapTabView.swift 71-73 calls `fitCameraToNearbyBathrooms()` with no check first. - That function (93-100) writes `cameraPosition = .region(fitted)` whenever any filtered bathroom exists. - In ClusteredMapView `apply` (115-137), a `.region` position calls `setUserTrackingMode(.none)` and then `setRegion`. Follow mode is switched off and the saved pan and zoom are replaced. - The coordinator's `regionDidChangeAnimated` (around 162-177) keeps the binding current, and its comment says the point is that "the map comes back where the user left it after the List tab". onAppear defeats that intent. Cold launch: `nearbyBathrooms` (40-46) returns every `filteredBathrooms` entry when `userLocation` is nil. The initial `.userLocation` follow position from ContentView 34-37 then gets replaced by a region fitted to every bathroom, wherever they are. Even when no bathroom is within 50 km, line 94 falls back to all filtered bathrooms, so a distant fit can also happen once location is known. Corrections: - The cited lines are accurate. - Severity "high" overstates it. No data is lost and nothing crashes; this is a camera and user-experience regression. Medium fits better. - The described effects only happen once at least one Bathroom matches the current filter (the guard on line 95). On the fix: a session flag in ContentView, such as `hasFittedCamera`, works. A simpler option is to drop the onAppear fit entirely and keep only the empty/non-empty onChange, since the launch default already follows the user.
  • CONFIRM (impact lens, high confidence): The finding is real, but I'd rate it medium, not high: it is a navigation annoyance with no data loss or crash. What I read: - Pitstop/Pitstop/MapTabView.swift: the onAppear at lines 71-73 calls fitCameraToNearbyBathrooms() with no guard. The function at lines 93-100 sets cameraPosition = .region(...) whenever the filtered bathroom list is not empty. - Pitstop/Pitstop/ContentView.swift, lines 51-66: `switch selectedView` inside a Group, so MapTabView is removed and rebuilt on every Map/List switch and onAppear runs again. ContentView's own comment (line 5) says it "Owns camera and filter state so both survive Map/List switches". The initial mapCameraPosition is .userLocation(followsHeading: false, fallback: .region(.unitedStates)). - Pitstop/Pitstop/ClusteredMapView.swift: apply() at line 115 calls setUserTrackingMode(.none) for non-user-location positions (line 130). The regionDidChangeAnimated comment at lines 162-163 says the map should come back where the user left it after the List tab, still following if it was. The method body runs from about line 164 to 177. History: the onAppear fit dates from the initial commit 6976afe. The binding-preservation code came later in 79421c0 ("Cluster map pins with an MKMapView"). So the old onAppear fit cancels the newer, documented behavior. That makes it a real contradiction, not a deliberate design. The onChange comment "Re-fit only when the list goes between empty and non-empty" points the same way: fits were meant to be rare. Cold launch claim: confirmed. If locationManager.userLocation is nil, nearbyBathrooms returns all of filteredBathrooms (lines 41-43), so the fit can frame pins in far-off cities, and the .userLocation follow mode is replaced. Nothing else mitigates it. It is not in the excluded list, does not duplicate the recent fixes, and no test covers it (map camera is verified by hand by design, so I am not asking for a test). Corrections: - The ClusteredMapView comment is at lines 162-163; the method body is about 164-177. - Severity: the impact is losing pan/zoom/follow mode on a tab switch and a bad first frame at launch, so medium. The proposed fix is reasonable. The simplest version is a hasFittedCamera flag kept in ContentView, or just not fitting when cameraPosition is still .userLocation. Moving the flag into MapTabView @State would not work, because that view is rebuilt on every switch.

CandidateDetailView shows "Not open 24 hours" for the 888 Candidates whose hours are unknown

Pitstop/Pitstop/CandidateDetailView.swift:58-64 · reviewer: detail-views-ui-state
What it is
The Hours section of the Candidate detail sheet branches only on `candidate.isOpen24Hours`. That field is false both when the seed says the store closes and when the seed has no hours at all (`openingHours == nil`). The model already has `isOpen24HoursIfKnown`, which returns nil for unknown hours, and the emergency ranking uses it (ContentView.swift:166). The detail view does not. In the bundled seed (version 4), 888 of 6055 records have `openingHours` null, so about 15% of Candidates state as fact that they are not open 24 hours.
Why it matters
The app treats unknown hours as unknown everywhere else (commit 571273b skips the late-night penalty for them), but the detail sheet tells the user a place closes when the data doesn't say so. At night, the user may skip a store that is actually open.
How to fix
Switch on `candidate.isOpen24HoursIfKnown`: `true` gives "Open 24 hours", `false` gives "Not open 24 hours", `nil` gives "Hours unknown" with a neutral icon such as `clock.badge.questionmark`. The same three states could also drive the clock icon in CandidateRow.
Expected improvement
The detail sheet matches the data and the ranking logic, and stops claiming a store closes when the hours are missing.
Evidence
text: candidate.isOpen24Hours ? "Open 24 hours" : "Not open 24 hours"
...
var isOpen24HoursIfKnown: Bool? {
    openingHours == nil ? nil : isOpen24Hours
}
Verifier notes (2)

EmergencyRanker.swift:152 uses `== false` there, which matches the 571273b design. - CandidateImporter keeps `openingHours` as nullable, so the nil state survives the import. - I parsed the bundled Pitstop/Pitstop/CandidateSeed.json: version 4, 6055 records, 888 with `openingHours` null. All 888 have `isOpen24Hours: false` (for example atp:bp:US-119999, a BP in Virginia Beach). So for about 15% of Candidates, the sheet presents "not open 24 hours" as fact when the data has no hours. Corrections: - The row suggestion is overstated. CandidateRow.swift:56 only draws a clock icon when the value is true and shows nothing otherwise, so the row never claims a place closes. Adding an "unknown" state there is optional. Related, not part of this finding: - The 24-hour filter (BathroomFilter.swift:135) already excludes unknown-hours Candidates, which is conservative and correct. - DetailView.swift:67 shows "Standard hours" for Bathrooms. Bathrooms have no unknown state, so it is out of scope. Severity: I suggest low rather than medium. It is a display-only wording problem: ranking and filtering already handle unknown hours correctly, and no data is wrong. The proposed fix (switch on `isOpen24HoursIfKnown`, with nil showing "Hours unknown") is correct and small.

One correction to the fix: CandidateRow.swift:56 only shows a clock when isOpen24Hours is true. It never says "not open", so the row is not misleading. Changing it is optional. The fix proposed for the detail view is right and small.
mediumConfirmedverifier suggests: low frontend effort: smallfound independently by 3 reviewers

Cancel during an in-flight save still inserts the Bathroom, leaving the Candidate sheet open so the same store can be logged twice

Pitstop/Pitstop/AddBathroomView.swift:277-283, 796-839 · reviewer: candidate-visit-link-seam
What it is
save() sets isSaving, then awaits reverseGeocode(selectedCoordinate) whenever the pin is more than 10 m from the Candidate's stored address or the Candidate has no address. The insert happens only after that await, and the await has no timeout. isSaving disables Save but not Cancel. If the user taps Cancel during the await, handleCancel() calls dismiss() at once, because isDirty ignores pin moves (see the separate finding). The fullScreenCover then closes, and CandidateDetailView's onDismiss runs `if isLinked { dismiss() }`. isLinked is still false because nothing has been inserted yet, so the Candidate sheet stays open. The detached Task then resumes, writes the photos, sets `bathroom.sourceID = candidate?.sourceID`, and inserts. The result is a Bathroom the user believes they cancelled, plus a sheet that still offers "I went here" for a Candidate that is now hidden on the map. Tapping it again creates a second Bathroom with the same sourceID. Bathroom.sourceID has no uniqueness constraint, and save() never checks for an existing link. The Discard alert path has the same race.
Why it matters
The user gets a phantom saved visit they cancelled and possibly a duplicate linked Bathroom. All of it lives in the only copy of user data. The race is easiest to hit offline or on a weak signal, where the reverse geocode stalls, and that is common when hunting for a bathroom on the road.
How to fix
Disable Cancel while isSaving (`.disabled(isSaving)` on the Cancel button), or make save() return early after its await when the view was cancelled, for example by checking a `didCancel` flag or using a cancellable stored Task that handleCancel cancels. Also, before inserting in candidate mode, run a fetchCount for an existing Bathroom with the same sourceID and skip the link (or the insert) if one exists.
Expected improvement
Cancel reliably means nothing is saved, the Candidate sheet's close-on-link logic always sees the final state, and one Candidate can only produce one linked Bathroom.
Evidence
Button("Cancel") { handleCancel() }
...
Button("Save") { Task { await save() } }
    .disabled(!canSave)
...
finalAddress = await reverseGeocode(selectedCoordinate) ?? (isEditMode ? editing?.address : nil)
...
bathroom.sourceID       = candidate?.sourceID
modelContext.insert(bathroom)
Verifier notes (2)
  • CONFIRM (existence lens, high confidence): I read /Users/daniel/Projects/pitstop/Pitstop/Pitstop/AddBathroomView.swift (lines 85-120, 270-300, 765-845) and /Users/daniel/Projects/pitstop/Pitstop/Pitstop/CandidateDetailView.swift (lines 60-145). Every claim checks out in the current code. - **Cancel stays live during a save:** the Cancel button at line 278 calls handleCancel() with no `.disabled`. Only Save is disabled (`canSave` includes `!isSaving`, line 88). - **Pin moves don't count as changes:** handleCancel (792-794) calls dismiss() straight away when !isDirty. In candidate mode, isDirty (115-119) never looks at selectedCoordinate. - **The geocode wait:** save() (796-839) sets isSaving and cancels geocodeTask. If candidateAddress(at:) returns nil (no address on the Candidate, or the pin is more than 10 m away, lines 784-789), it awaits reverseGeocode with no timeout (772-779). - **The insert happens anyway:** after the await, save() writes photos, sets `bathroom.sourceID = candidate?.sourceID` and calls `modelContext.insert(bathroom)` (834-835). Nothing checks for a cancel or an existing link first. - **The save task is never cancelled:** it's an unstructured `Task { await save() }` started from the button (281), so closing the view doesn't stop it. Even a cancelled task wouldn't help, because reverseGeocode uses `try?` and returns nil, and the insert still runs. - **The Candidate sheet stays open:** CandidateDetailView line 108 runs `onDismiss: { if isLinked { dismiss() } }`. isLinked (139-143) is a fetchCount on sourceID, so it's false while the save is still in flight. The "I went here" button (75) is always shown, and nothing makes sourceID unique. A second save therefore creates a duplicate linked Bathroom. - **The Discard path has the same race:** the alert's Discard button (291) just calls dismiss(). Corrections and scope: - The race is broader than candidate mode. In plain add mode the reverse geocode always runs, so Cancel during the wait still inserts a Bathroom the user cancelled. In edit mode the edits are applied after Cancel. - "Offline" is a bit overstated. With no network, the reverse geocode probably fails fast. A weak signal is the realistic way to stall it. - The line numbers are correct. Better fix: store the save Task and gate on it. Either disable Cancel while isSaving, or set a didCancel flag in handleCancel and the Discard button and return early after the await. Also add a fetchCount check on sourceID before the candidate-mode insert.
  • CONFIRM (impact lens, high confidence): I read /Users/daniel/Projects/pitstop/Pitstop/Pitstop/AddBathroomView.swift (isDirty at lines 90-120, toolbar at 276-295, reverseGeocode at 772-779, candidateAddress at 783-789, handleCancel and save at 792-839) and CandidateDetailView.swift (the fullScreenCover at line 108 and isLinked at 139-143). The race is real in the current code: - The Cancel button at line 278 has no `.disabled(isSaving)`. Only Save is disabled through canSave. - In save(), the only await is `reverseGeocode` at line 804. It runs when the pin is more than 10 m from the Candidate's stored address, or when the Candidate has no address. The insert at line 835 comes after that await, and nothing checks for cancellation after it. - handleCancel() calls dismiss() right away when isDirty is false. If isDirty is true, the Discard button (line 291) also calls dismiss() right away. Nothing has been inserted yet at that point, so the sheet stays open. Nothing blocks a second save: save() has no check for an existing link, and Bathroom.sourceID has no uniqueness constraint. A weak signal is the more realistic case. - The result is an extra record the user can delete. Nothing is lost. On the fix: `.disabled(isSaving)` on Cancel is the minimal correct fix, and the same guard should cover the Discard path. A guard against an existing link at insert time is a reasonable extra safety net.
mediumConfirmedverifier suggests: low frontend effort: smallfound independently by 2 reviewers

Map tab ignores the distance filter for Bathrooms but applies it to Candidates, contradicting the spec and the List tab

Pitstop/Pitstop/MapTabView.swift:26-37 · reviewer: filters-and-list
What it is
With a Max Distance chip selected, `filteredBathrooms` on the map only calls `filter.applies(to:)` and never `isWithinMaxDistance`, while `filteredCandidates` checks both. ListTabView checks distance for both types (ListTabView.swift lines 46-55). So at "1 mi" the map still shows a visited bathroom 20 miles away but hides an unvisited Target 2 miles away, and the List tab shows neither. The comment's reasoning (pins appearing and disappearing as the user moves is confusing) also applies to the Candidate pins, which do appear and disappear now.
Why it matters
The spec says "The distance filter and the 24-hour filter apply to both types", and user story 11 asks that "within 1 mile means the same thing everywhere". The filter sheet copy says "Only shows places within the selected radius." The map now applies one radius rule to one pin type and another rule to the other, and it disagrees with the List tab.
How to fix
Apply `filter.isWithinMaxDistance($0.coordinate, of: locationManager.userLocation)` in `filteredBathrooms` as ListTabView already does. Or, if Bathrooms really should be exempt on the map, exempt Candidates too and change the filter sheet copy to say distance is list-only. Either way, put the predicate in one shared helper (for example `BathroomFilter.visible(bathrooms:candidates:userLocation:)`) so Map and List cannot drift apart again.
Expected improvement
One radius rule for both place types on both tabs, matching the spec and the filter sheet text.
Evidence
/// Skips the max-distance filter: pins appearing and disappearing as the user moves is confusing on a map.
/// Candidates still honor it ...
private var filteredBathrooms: [Bathroom] {
    guard filter.isActive else { return allBathrooms }
    return allBathrooms.filter { filter.applies(to: $0) }
}
Verifier notes (3)

Then read ListTabView.swift lines 41-55. `displayedPlaces` checks `isWithinMaxDistance` for both Bathrooms (lines 46-50) and Candidates (lines 51-55), so the finding's line numbers are correct. FilterSheetView.swift line 27 reads "Requires location access. Only shows places within the selected radius." The message for ec79ac7 says "Bathrooms on the map still skip it, as on main." Even so, the stated intentional design says "distance and 24-hour filters apply to both types," so the map exemption breaks that design rather than being part of it. The suggested fix (add `isWithinMaxDistance` to `filteredBathrooms`, or share one predicate helper between Map and List) is sound.

The finding's point that the "confusing pins" reasoning also covers Candidate pins is fair. README.md line 16 ("Filters apply live to both the map and the list") and the filter sheet copy ("Only shows places within the selected radius.") are a little loose about the map exemption. That is at most an info-level documentation note, for example a copy tweak or a comment. The shared-helper suggestion is a reasonable refactor, but it is not needed to fix any defect. The severity of medium is inflated, because no data is affected and the user picked this behavior. Correction: the line range 26-37 is right.

Unlinking a Bathroom revives a Candidate for a store the current seed has already dropped

Pitstop/Pitstop/CandidateImporter.swift:76-78 · reviewer: seed-import-correctness
What it is
apply() keeps a Candidate that is missing from the seed when a Bathroom links to it. The Candidate stays, with its old seedVersion. If the user later deletes that Bathroom (ListTabView.deleteBathroom / DetailView), nothing links to the Candidate anymore and unlinked(from:) stops hiding it. A store that the current seed says no longer exists then shows up again as an unvisited Candidate on the map, in the list, and in emergency ranking. The import only runs when the bundled seed version goes up, so nothing removes it until the next seed ships. seedVersion is written but nothing reads it (grep finds it only in the importer, the model and previews), so the app cannot tell the Candidate is stale.
Why it matters
The emergency button can send the user to a closed store, which is the outcome the seed deletion step exists to prevent. The data stays wrong for as long as the installed seed version stays the same.
How to fix
Treat a Candidate whose seedVersion is below the last imported version, and that is not dismissed, as retired. One option: hide or delete it when its last linking Bathroom is deleted. The other: filter it out wherever unlinked(from:) is applied, by comparing seedVersion to UserDefaults' candidateSeedLastImportedVersion. Add a test: link, drop from the seed, delete the Bathroom, and expect the Candidate to be gone or hidden.
Expected improvement
Closed stores never come back after a visit is deleted. seedVersion gets a real use, and the 'deletes missing Candidates unless dismissed or linked' rule still holds once the link goes away.
Evidence
for candidate in existing.values where !candidate.isDismissed && !linkedIDs.contains(candidate.sourceID) {
            context.delete(candidate)
        }
Verifier notes (2)
  • CONFIRM (existence lens, high confidence): I read /Users/daniel/Projects/pitstop/Pitstop/Pitstop/CandidateImporter.swift. Deleting a Bathroom does nothing to the Candidate it was linked to. ListTabView.swift:243-247 and DetailView.swift:261-265 only delete the images and the Bathroom. Hiding is computed on the fly, so the Candidate comes back as soon as the Bathroom is gone: - `Sequence<Candidate>.unlinked(from:)` (Candidate.swift:73-76) is used by ListTabView.swift:51 and MapTabView.swift:34. - EmergencyRanker.swift:142-149 does its own linkedIDs filter instead of calling unlinked(from:), so the finding's mention of unlinked(from:) for emergency ranking is slightly off. The outcome is the same: once the link is gone, the Candidate is eligible again if it is not dismissed. I grepped for seedVersion. In the app it is only written: in the importer, the model, and preview initializers. Tests assert on it. Nothing compares it to candidateSeedLastImportedVersion, so the staleness claim holds. Filtering at display time would have to be repeated at each call site, including EmergencyRanker. The suggested regression test is sound.

mediumConfirmedverifier suggests: low emergency-ranking effort: medium

Logging a visit from an unknown-hours Candidate makes it rank 20 minutes worse at night

Pitstop/Pitstop/EmergencyRanker.swift:144-153 · reviewer: emergency-ranking
What it is
The ranker treats a Candidate's hours as tri-state (`isOpen24Hours == false` means known to close, nil means unknown and skips the late-night penalty, per commit 571273b). A Bathroom's hours are a plain Bool and the ranker reads `!isOpen24Hours` as "known to close at night". When the user logs a visit from a Candidate whose seed has no hours, AddBathroomView seeds the toggle from `c.isOpen24Hours` (false by default, line 156), so the new Bathroom stores `isOpen24Hours = false`. Every Bathroom whose user left the toggle at its default is likewise treated as closing at night.
Why it matters
Visiting and rating a place should never make the emergency button less likely to pick it. Take an unknown-hours Wawa 5 minutes away. Unvisited, it scores 5 + 3 (reliable) = 8 effective minutes at 2am. After the user logs it as a 5-star Bathroom it scores 5 + 0 + 20 = 25, so at night an unvisited uncertain gamble 14 minutes away now beats it. This is the reverse of the user stories that say a known good bathroom should beat a gamble.
How to fix
Make the Bathroom's hours unknown-capable for ranking purposes. Options: (a) add an optional hours-known field to Bathroom through a lightweight migration and pass `Bool?` into `VisitedPlace.isOpen24Hours`, mapping the default/unset state to nil. (b) Carry the Candidate's unknown-hours state across when converting, for example by leaving the toggle untouched and storing nil. Then use the same `== false` test for both types in `eligible()`.
Expected improvement
Visited places get the same unknown-hours treatment as Candidates, so logging a visit never adds a 20-minute night penalty the unvisited version did not have.
Evidence
EmergencyRanker.swift:146 `penalty: Penalty.visited(rating: $0.rating), closesAtNight: !$0.isOpen24Hours)` vs :152 `closesAtNight: $0.isOpen24Hours == false)`; AddBathroomView.swift:156 `_isOpen24Hours      = State(initialValue: c.isOpen24Hours)`; Bathroom.swift:90 `isOpen24Hours:          Bool           = false,`
Verifier notes (2)

Any fix that changes how the ranker treats Bathroom hours needs the optional field plus a migration, which the store invariant allows.

Emergency button routes to the best place at any distance; no "nothing in range" outcome

Pitstop/Pitstop/ContentView.swift:177-190 · reviewer: emergency-ranking
What it is
The handler shows an alert only when the ranked list is empty. Ranking has no distance or effective-minutes cap, so whenever at least one Bathroom or Candidate exists anywhere, `ranked.first` is opened in Apple Maps no matter how far away it is. Spec user story 41 says "I want the alert to say clearly when nothing is in range, so that I know to look elsewhere right away."
Why it matters
The seed covers only VA/MD/DC. Outside that region, for example on a road trip, the emergency button quietly starts driving directions to a store hours away. That is worse than an alert telling the user to look locally.
How to fix
Add a tunable cap next to the penalty table (for example `maxEffectiveMinutes` or a straight-line radius). Have `rank` drop places beyond it, or check `best.effectiveMinutes` in the handler, and show the existing alert with copy saying nothing is in range.
Expected improvement
Matches story 41. Users far from any known place get a clear alert instead of directions to a far-away destination.
Evidence
`guard let best = ranked.first else {
    emergencyAlertMsg = "No saved bathrooms or unvisited places to go to. ..."` followed by `MapURLHelper.openDirections(toLatitude: best.latitude, longitude: best.longitude, mode: .driving)`

It applies no distance cap and no effective-minutes cap. `nearest(8, ...)` only limits which places get driving-time lookups. Spec check: closed issue #2 has user story 41: "As a driver, I want the alert to say clearly when nothing is in range, so that I know to look elsewhere right away." Emergency origin may be a stale or low-accuracy fix: LocationManager drops the timestamp and accuracy

Pitstop/Pitstop/LocationManager.swift:9, 56-59 · reviewer: emergency-ranking
What it is
`didUpdateLocations` stores only `location.coordinate`. The CLLocation timestamp and horizontalAccuracy are thrown away and `userLocation` is never cleared. With when-in-use authorization, updates are suspended while the app is in the background, so after the user drives somewhere and reopens the app, `userLocation` still holds the old coordinate until a new fix arrives. The first fix delivered after startUpdatingLocation can also be a cached one. `handleEmergencyTap` accepts any non-nil coordinate as the origin for both the 8-nearest preselect and the driving-ETA requests.
Why it matters
The emergency flow is most likely to be used right after opening the app mid-drive, which is when the stored coordinate is most likely to be miles out of date. The ranker would then pick the best place near where the user used to be.
How to fix
Store the full CLLocation (or its timestamp and horizontalAccuracy) in LocationManager. In `handleEmergencyTap`, treat a fix older than about 30-60 seconds or with poor accuracy as unavailable: await a fresh fix (for example `CLLocationUpdate.liveUpdates()` or `requestLocation()` with a short timeout), or fall back to the existing "Waiting for your location" alert.
Expected improvement
Emergency ranking always starts from the user's current position, not from wherever the app last ran.
Evidence
`func locationManager(_ manager: CLLocationManager, didUpdateLocations locations: [CLLocation]) {
        guard let location = locations.last else { return }
        userLocation = location.coordinate
    }`; ContentView.swift:154 `guard let userCoord = locationManager.userLocation else {`
Verifier notes (2)
  • CONFIRM (existence lens, high confidence): I read /Users/daniel/Projects/pitstop/Pitstop/Pitstop/LocationManager.swift (all of it) and ContentView.swift lines 140-190. The finding holds. Line 9 declares `var userLocation: CLLocationCoordinate2D?`. Lines 56-59 store only `location.coordinate` and throw away the timestamp and horizontalAccuracy. Nothing ever sets userLocation back to nil. Updates start once (the isUpdatingLocation guard) and nothing handles scenePhase. handleEmergencyTap is at line 144, and its guard is at line 154 (`guard let userCoord = locationManager.userLocation else {`), not 153. That guard accepts any non-nil coordinate and passes it to EmergencyRanker.nearest(8, ...), DrivingTimeLookup.drivingTimes, and EmergencyRanker.rank. Why I suggest lowering severity to low: with when-in-use authorization, standard location updates resume automatically when the app comes back to the foreground, and a fresh fix usually arrives within a second or two. So the stale window is short. The user only hits it by tapping Emergency right away. Also, MapURLHelper.openDirections opens Apple Maps, which routes from the device's live position, so the directions themselves are correct. Only the choice of destination could be off, and only during that window. The cached-first-fix concern is real but has the same short window. A simpler fix than the one proposed: store the CLLocation, and in handleEmergencyTap treat a fix older than about 30 seconds or with horizontalAccuracy above a few hundred meters as unavailable. Show the existing "Waiting for your location" alert, or await one `requestLocation()` with a short timeout. This does not duplicate any excluded item or recent fix.
  • CONFIRM (impact lens, medium confidence): I read all of /Users/daniel/Projects/pitstop/Pitstop/Pitstop/LocationManager.swift and lines 130-195 of /Users/daniel/Projects/pitstop/Pitstop/Pitstop/ContentView.swift, then searched the app sources. Coarse accuracy (a few hundred meters) barely changes which place ranks best, so the timestamp is what matters more than accuracy. Rating it low rather than medium: this is a real gap in the code, but in normal use the stale window is a second or two.
mediumConfirmedverifier suggests: low map effort: small

Tapping a tight cluster opens only an arbitrary first member; co-located places are unreachable from the map

Pitstop/Pitstop/ClusteredMapView.swift:179-193 · reviewer: map-clustering
What it is
`open(_:on:)` checks whether a cluster's members span less than 30 m. If they do, it calls `parent.onSelect` with `members.first` and returns without zooming. `memberAnnotations` has no defined order, so which place opens can change between taps. The other members are never offered. Places at the same or nearly the same coordinate still collide at maximum zoom with `collisionMode = .circle`, so those clusters never split. Their other members cannot be opened from the map at all. Members 10 to 30 m apart would split if the map zoomed in, but tapping them opens one sheet instead of zooming.
Why it matters
Two Bathrooms logged in the same building (two floors of a mall, an airport concourse), or two seed Candidates at one address, show up as a "2" cluster. Tapping it always opens one of them, and the other cannot be reached from the Map tab. A badge that promises more places than the user can open reads as broken.
How to fix
When the members are too close to split, present a chooser instead of picking `first`. For example, add a `.cluster([MapSelection])` case to the selection, or show a confirmation dialog listing the members' names, then open the chosen one. Otherwise sort members deterministically (visited first, then by name). For spans that are small but would still split, zoom in further, using `setCamera` with a small `centerCoordinateDistance`, rather than opening the first member.
Expected improvement
Every pin on the map can be opened, and cluster taps behave the same way every time.
Evidence
if max(rect.size.width, rect.size.height) / pointsPerMeter < 30 {
                if let first = members.first as? PlaceAnnotation {
                    parent.onSelect(first.place)
                }
                return
            }

ImageStorage.resized renders at screen scale, so stored photos are 3x the intended pixel size

Pitstop/Pitstop/ImageStorage.swift:170-186 · reviewer: images-and-photos
What it is
resized(toMaxDimension:) computes a target size in points and draws it with UIGraphicsImageRenderer(size:), which uses the default format. That format's scale is the main screen scale (3 on current iPhones), so the bitmap comes out at newSize times 3 in pixels. Camera and picker images have scale 1, which means size equals pixel size. A 4032x3024 photo meant to become a 1200px full image is stored at about 3600x2700, and the 200px thumbnail is stored at about 600x450. fullMaxDimension and thumbMaxDimension therefore do not cap anything in pixels.
Why it matters
Each stored JPEG is about 9x the intended pixel count. Disk use grows that much, every thumbnail decode in the list, carousel and edit form decodes a 600px bitmap instead of a 200px one, and each full image in fullImageCache costs about 39 MB of decoded memory instead of about 4 MB. The caches have no countLimit or totalCostLimit, so they rely only on NSCache's memory-pressure eviction.
How to fix
Create a UIGraphicsImageRendererFormat with format.scale = 1 (or use format = .init(); format.scale = 1) and pass it to UIGraphicsImageRenderer(size: newSize, format: format). Optionally set a totalCostLimit on both caches and pass the pixel byte count as the cost.
Expected improvement
Full images are stored at 1200px and thumbnails at 200px as the constants say. That cuts per-photo disk use and decode memory by about 9x and makes list scrolling and the photo viewer lighter.
Evidence
return UIGraphicsImageRenderer(size: newSize).image { _ in
            draw(in: CGRect(origin: .zero, size: newSize))
        }
Verifier notes (2)
  • CONFIRM (existence lens, high confidence): I read /Users/daniel/Projects/pitstop/Pitstop/Pitstop/ImageStorage.swift. Lines 11-12 set fullMaxDimension = 1200 and thumbMaxDimension = 200. Lines 16-18 create both NSCaches with no countLimit or totalCostLimit. write() at lines 57-75 calls image.resized(...) and then jpegData. The private UIImage extension at lines 170-187 computes newSize in points from `size` and returns `UIGraphicsImageRenderer(size: newSize).image { ... }`. No UIGraphicsImageRendererFormat is passed anywhere (grep found none), so the renderer uses the default format, whose scale is the main screen scale. The output bitmap is therefore newSize times the screen scale in pixels, and jpegData writes those pixel dimensions to disk. The inputs have scale 1. AddBathroomView.swift:732 builds them with UIImage(data:), and camera images also come in at scale 1. So on a 3x device a 4032x3024 photo is stored at about 3600x2700, and the thumbnail at about 600x450, as the finding claims. Correction: the cited range 170-186 is close. The function is at 173-186 and the renderer call is at 183. The fix is correct: create a UIGraphicsImageRendererFormat, set scale = 1, and pass it to the renderer. Existing photos on disk stay oversized unless they are re-encoded. Setting a cost limit on the caches is a reasonable extra.

BathroomRow thumbnail task is keyed on bathroom.id, so it goes stale after photos are edited

Pitstop/Pitstop/BathroomRow.swift:29-34 · reviewer: detail-views-ui-state
What it is
The row loads its thumbnail in `.task(id: bathroom.id)`. The id never changes when the bathroom is edited. AddBathroomView's edit path (AddBathroomView.swift:808-818) deletes removed files, evicts them from the thumbnail cache, and rewrites `bathroom.imageFileNames`, but the row's task does not run again. Adding a first photo to a bathroom that had none leaves the toilet placeholder in the row. Removing or replacing the first photo keeps showing the old image held in `@State thumbnailImage`, even though its file has been deleted from disk. Edits start from DetailView, which is a sheet over the List tab, so the row underneath stays alive and keeps its stale state.
Why it matters
After the user edits photos, the list shows a thumbnail that doesn't match the bathroom's photos, possibly one already deleted, until the row happens to be recreated.
How to fix
Key the task on the first file name as well, for example `.task(id: bathroom.imageFileNames.first)`, or on a small struct holding `bathroom.id` and the first file name. Keep the existing nil branch so the placeholder comes back when all photos are removed.
Expected improvement
The list thumbnail updates as soon as the edit is saved.
Evidence
.task(id: bathroom.id) {
    guard let fileName = bathroom.imageFileNames.first else {
        thumbnailImage = nil; return
    }

FlowLayout.placeSubviews reuses row assignments computed for a different width

Pitstop/Pitstop/FlowLayout.swift:31, 41-43, 56 · reviewer: detail-views-ui-state
What it is
`sizeThatFits` recomputes the cache for whatever width it is proposed, with nil treated as infinity. `placeSubviews` recomputes only when the subview count changes, never when `bounds.width` differs from the width the cache was built for. SwiftUI can call `sizeThatFits` several times with different proposals (ideal size with nil, then the real width, sometimes in either order), so whichever call ran last decides the row breaks. If that call had nil or a wider width, every chip is placed on one row and runs past the container's edge. Separately, subviews are placed with `.unspecified`, so a chip wider than the container is never given the container width and gets clipped instead of wrapping or truncating. The comment on line 73 says a wide chip sits alone on its own row, but nothing limits how far it overflows.
Why it matters
The filter sheet's chip rows (FilterSheetView.swift lines 137-205) can show chips overlapping the edge or a wrong height, depending on SwiftUI's internal call order. That kind of layout bug is intermittent and hard to reproduce, and more likely at large Dynamic Type sizes.
How to fix
Store the width the cache was computed for (for example `cache.width`) and recompute in `placeSubviews` whenever it differs from `bounds.width`. Measure and place each subview with `ProposedViewSize(width: min(size.width, bounds.width), height: nil)` so a chip wider than the container is constrained to it.
Expected improvement
Row breaks always match the width the chips are actually placed in, and oversized chips stay inside the container.
Evidence
if cache.sizes.count != subviews.count {
    compute(in: bounds.width, subviews: subviews, cache: &cache)
}
...
subview.place(at: CGPoint(x: x, y: rowOriginY), proposal: .unspecified)
Verifier notes (2)
  • CONFIRM (existence lens, medium confidence): I read /Users/daniel/Projects/pitstop/Pitstop/Pitstop/FlowLayout.swift (110 lines) and FilterSheetView.swift lines 125-210. The code matches the finding and the cited lines are right. Line 31: `sizeThatFits` runs `compute(in: proposal.width ?? .infinity, ...)` every time it is called and overwrites the shared cache. Lines 41-43: `placeSubviews` recomputes only when `cache.sizes.count != subviews.count`. It never compares `bounds.width` with the width the cache was built for, and the Cache struct (lines 15-20) does not record that width at all. Line 56: subviews are placed with `.unspecified`. Line 64 also measures them with `.unspecified`, so a chip is never limited to the container's width. The comment on lines 73-74 only promises that a too-wide chip gets its own row. Nothing stops it from running past the edge. Scope, corrected: FlowLayout is used only in FilterSheetView.swift, at lines 137, 152, 173, 189 and 205 (the distance, access, stall, gender and wait-time chip rows). The finding's "137-205" range is accurate. Severity: I suggest low rather than medium. Apple's Layout documentation says placeSubviews should not assume the last sizeThatFits call used the same proposal, so the stale-cache risk is real. In practice, though, SwiftUI usually measures with the final proposal right before placing, so this is a latent fragility, not a bug anyone has observed. The overflow case needs a single chip label wider than the sheet. With short labels like distance names and wait times, that is unlikely except at the largest accessibility text sizes. The proposed fix is sound. Store the cache width and recompute when `bounds.width` differs. Then measure and place each subview with a width proposal capped at the container width; that cap has to go into `compute` (line 64) as well as `place`, or the row math stays wrong.
  • CONFIRM (impact lens, medium confidence): I read /Users/daniel/Projects/pitstop/Pitstop/Pitstop/FlowLayout.swift in full, plus the five call sites in /Users/daniel/Projects/pitstop/Pitstop/Pitstop/FilterSheetView.swift (lines 137, 152, 173, 189, 205). The chip rows sit in a vertical ScrollView, near line 11. The line numbers and evidence in the finding are accurate. The code does what the finding says: - Line 31: `sizeThatFits` runs `compute` for whatever width it is proposed, treating nil as infinity. - Lines 41-43: `placeSubviews` only recomputes when the subview count changes. It never checks whether `bounds.width` matches the width the cache was built for. So placement reuses the row assignments from whichever `sizeThatFits` call ran last. - Line 56: subviews are placed with `.unspecified`. - Line 64: subviews are also measured with `.unspecified`. The Layout protocol does not promise that the last `sizeThatFits` proposal equals the final bounds, so this is a real latent bug. Apple's own flow-layout sample avoids it by recomputing rows from `bounds` inside `placeSubviews`. The impact is overstated, which is why I suggest low rather than medium: 1. In practice, SwiftUI usually calls `sizeThatFits` with the final proposal right before it calls `placeSubviews`, so the bad ordering is uncommon. 2. All five rows use short labels from enum displayName/shortName values on a single sheet. No user-generated text goes through them. The worst case is a cosmetic overflow in the filter sheet, with no data or behavior effect. 3. The point about a chip wider than the container is technically true. But it only shows up at extreme Dynamic Type sizes with these short labels. Better fix: the simplest robust version is to always recompute from `bounds.width` in `placeSubviews`. Measuring a handful of chips is cheap, so the cache adds little. Otherwise, store `cache.width` and compare it, as the finding proposes. Clamping each proposal to `min(size.width, bounds.width)` is reasonable, but it has to be applied in `compute` as well as in `place`. Otherwise the measured sizes and the placed sizes disagree.
mediumConfirmed frontend effort: medium

Zoomed photo cannot be panned, and horizontal swipes still page away while zoomed

Pitstop/Pitstop/PhotoViewerView.swift:105-129 · reviewer: photo-viewer-camera-ui
What it is
ZoomablePhotoPage applies only .scaleEffect(currentZoom), which scales around the view's center. There is no offset state and no drag gesture to pan the zoomed image, so after pinching in, the user can only see the center region. The edges are cropped off-screen and cannot be reached. The TabView paging also stays enabled while zoomed: there is no .scrollDisabled(isZoomed) or similar, so a one-finger horizontal drag that the user means as a pan switches pages instead, and onChange(of: selectedIndex) then resets the zoom. The only zoom-out path is a double tap, which only resets and never zooms in.
Why it matters
Pinch-to-zoom is the reason this viewer exists: users zoom to read signs, door codes or posted hours in a bathroom photo. If the text sits off-center they can never see it, and trying to drag to it throws them onto the next photo.
How to fix
Add @State offset plus a DragGesture that is active only when zoomScale > 1, applied as .offset(...) after .scaleEffect and clamped to the scaled image bounds. Reset the offset in the double-tap handler and in onDisappear. Disable TabView paging while isZoomed is true (for example .scrollDisabled(isZoomed) on the TabView), or wrap each page in a UIScrollView-backed representable that gives you native zoom and pan. Optionally let a double tap zoom in to about 2x when the scale is 1.
Expected improvement
Users can inspect any part of a zoomed photo, and paging resumes only after they zoom back out.
Evidence

    .scaledToFit()
    .scaleEffect(currentZoom)

        MagnificationGesture()
...

LocationManager never checks accuracyAuthorization, so Approximate Location fixes are used as if precise

Pitstop/Pitstop/LocationManager.swift:51-59, 67-74 · reviewer: location-lifecycle-and-search
What it is
LocationManager only reads `authorizationStatus`. It never reads `manager.accuracyAuthorization`, never calls `requestTemporaryFullAccuracyAuthorization(withPurposeKey:)`, and has no reduced-accuracy flag. The project's Info.plist keys (pbxproj lines 335/373) only set NSLocationWhenInUseUsageDescription, with no NSLocationTemporaryUsageDescriptionDictionary. If the user turns off Precise Location in the permission prompt or in Settings, `didUpdateLocations` still writes the approximate coordinate (it can be several kilometers off) into `userLocation`, and the app shows no warning.
Why it matters
`userLocation` is where the pin starts in AddBathroomView when a Bathroom is added with the + button (`let start = userLocation ?? ...`), and the user may save it without dragging. It is also the origin for distance sorting and the distance filter in the List tab, and for the straight-line preselection of the 8 nearest places in the emergency flow. With an approximate fix, new Bathrooms get saved kilometers from where they really are, and the nearest-place ranking can be wrong. None of this is visible to the user.
How to fix
Expose `var isReducedAccuracy: Bool` and set it from `manager.accuracyAuthorization == .reducedAccuracy` in `locationManagerDidChangeAuthorization`. When it is true, add an NSLocationTemporaryUsageDescriptionDictionary purpose key and call `requestTemporaryFullAccuracyAuthorization(withPurposeKey:)` before the emergency lookup and when AddBathroomView opens. Otherwise show a banner, like LocationDeniedBanner, that links to Settings, and do not start the add-pin at an approximate userLocation.
Expected improvement
Users with Approximate Location get told about it, and they no longer save Bathrooms or get emergency rankings based on a fix that can be kilometers off.
Evidence
func locationManagerDidChangeAuthorization(_ manager: CLLocationManager) {
        authorizationStatus = manager.authorizationStatus
        evaluateAuthorization(manager.authorizationStatus)
    }
...
        guard let location = locations.last else { return }
        userLocation = location.coordinate

isDirty misses several fields, so Cancel discards real edits without asking

Pitstop/Pitstop/AddBathroomView.swift:90-120 · reviewer: add-edit-form
What it is
In add mode, isDirty ignores dateVisited, requiresReceiptCode, stallType, genderType, hasExtraToiletPaper, waitTime, dispenserRating, and the pin location. In edit mode it ignores selectedCoordinate, so a moved pin is not counted. It also compares dispenserRating but not hasDispenser: turning the dispenser on (saved as max(1, 0) = 1) or off while the rating is 0 does not count as a change. In each of these cases handleCancel dismisses immediately, with no Discard Changes alert.
Why it matters
A user who sets the stall type, gender, wait time, or a carefully placed pin and then taps Cancel by mistake loses that work with no confirmation. The form is long, so this is easy to hit.
How to fix
Build both branches from one snapshot: a value of all form fields (coordinate included) taken at init, compared with the current one. The add and edit branches can then never drift apart.
Expected improvement
Any change to any field triggers the Discard Changes confirmation.
Evidence

Rating sort tiebreak uses case-sensitive `<` while other sorts use localized case-insensitive compare

Pitstop/Pitstop/ListTabView.swift:216-221 · reviewer: filters-and-list
What it is
Within a rating tier, the Rating sort breaks ties with `$0.name < $1.name`, a byte-wise, case-sensitive comparison. Date Added and Name sorts use `localizedCaseInsensitiveCompare`. Every Candidate has rating 0, so the whole unvisited block (thousands of rows) and every unrated Bathroom is ordered by this tiebreak. Any lowercase or accented name (for example "7-Eleven" vs "sheetz", or "Épicerie") lands in a different place than it does under Name sort.
Why it matters
The same places come out in different relative orders under the Rating and Name sorts. That is a small but visible inconsistency across thousands of rows.
How to fix
Use `$0.name.localizedCaseInsensitiveCompare($1.name) == .orderedAscending` for the Rating tiebreak, ideally through one shared name comparator used by all three sorts.
Expected improvement
Ties sort the same way under every sort option.
Evidence
return list.sorted {
                if $0.rating == $1.rating { return $0.name < $1.name }
Verifier notes (2)
  • CONFIRM (existence lens, high confidence): The finding is real. I read /Users/daniel/Projects/pitstop/Pitstop/Pitstop/ListTabView.swift, lines 202-239 (the `sorted(_:)` function). - **The bug:** line 217 in the `.rating` case reads `if $0.rating == $1.rating { return $0.name < $1.name }`, which is a byte-wise, case-sensitive comparison. - **The other sorts:** the `.dateAdded` case (line 230, when both dates are nil) and the `.name` case (line 236) both use `localizedCaseInsensitiveCompare`. A grep shows line 217 is the only `.name <` comparison in the app sources, so the mismatch is real and the cited lines (216-221) are correct. **Correction:** the example "7-Eleven" vs "sheetz" does not show the bug. Digits sort before letters under both comparisons, so the order is the same. Examples that do show it: - "Sheetz" vs "arco": byte-wise puts "Sheetz" first because 'S' (0x53) < 'a' (0x61); the localized comparison puts "arco" first. - Accented names such as "Épicerie": byte-wise sorts them after every plain ASCII letter. **Scope:** the "thousands of rows" claim assumes Candidates have rating 0, which the comment at line 215 supports. But seed brand names are mostly capitalized, so the order of most rows will match Name sort. Only names that start lowercase or with an accent land in a different place. Low severity is right. **Fix:** the suggested fix is correct. Optionally, a shared comparator could be used by all three call sites.
  • CONFIRM (impact lens, high confidence): I read /Users/daniel/Projects/pitstop/Pitstop/Pitstop/ListTabView.swift: sorted(_:) at lines 202-238 and the private MapSelection sort-key extension at lines 252-282. The finding holds. Line 217 has `if $0.rating == $1.rating { return $0.name < $1.name }`, which is a plain String `<` and so case-sensitive and not locale-aware. The dateAdded nil/nil tiebreak (line 230) and the name sort (lines 235-236) both use `localizedCaseInsensitiveCompare`. MapSelection.rating returns 0 for every Candidate (line 273), so the whole Candidate block plus every unrated Bathroom is ordered by this tiebreak. I found no mitigation anywhere: no caller re-sorts, and nothing documents the difference as intended. Corrections: - Line range: the sort is lines 214-221; the faulty comparison is on line 217. - The "7-Eleven" vs "sheetz" example is wrong. Digits sort before letters under both comparisons, so that pair comes out in the same order either way. A correct example is "sheetz" vs "Wawa". Under `<`, every uppercase letter sorts before every lowercase one, so "Wawa" comes before "sheetz", while Name sort puts "sheetz" first. Accented first letters such as "Épicerie" also sort after "Z" under `<`. - Scope is narrower than "thousands of rows". Seed Candidate names are mostly capitalized brand names, which order the same under both comparisons. In practice the divergence hits user-typed Bathroom names and the odd lowercase or accented seed name. The proposed fix (localizedCaseInsensitiveCompare, ideally one shared name comparator for all three sorts) is correct and harmless. Severity low is appropriate. This does not duplicate any excluded or recently fixed item.

Dismissed Candidates cluster with unvisited ones and are announced as "unvisited places"

Pitstop/Pitstop/ClusteredMapView.swift:250-256, 327-345 · reviewer: map-clustering
What it is
`PinStyle.clusteringIdentifier` returns "candidate" for both `.candidate` and `.dismissed`, so dismissed places merge into unvisited clusters when Show dismissed is on. `ClusterPinView.kind(of:)` maps `.dismissed` to `.uncertain`. A cluster made up only of dismissed places therefore draws exactly like a cluster of uncertain unvisited places. VoiceOver reads it as "N unvisited places, zoom in", and the count includes places the user has marked as having no public restroom.
Why it matters
With Show dismissed on, cluster counts and colors overstate how many usable unvisited places are nearby. Places the user has ruled out look the same as live guesses until they zoom all the way in.
How to fix
Give `.dismissed` its own clustering identifier (for example "dismissed") and its own `ClusterAnnotation.Kind` (gray with the X badge), or leave dismissed members out of the candidate cluster's tint and count. Update the accessibility label to say "dismissed places" for that kind.

Cluster badges and labels match what the user will find inside them.
Evidence

...

Stale reverse-geocode result can overwrite the address for a newer pin position

Pitstop/Pitstop/AddBathroomView.swift:742-755 · reviewer: add-edit-form
What it is
scheduleGeocode cancels the previous task, but the cancelled task checks Task.isCancelled only before it starts the lookup. If the lookup is already awaiting `reverseGeocode` when the pin moves, the old task still assigns its result to geocodedAddress when it returns, and sets isGeocoding = false while the new task is still pending. The candidateAddress early return has the same problem: it sets the Candidate's address and returns, and an in-flight lookup can then replace it with the geocode of the previous position. The task is also never cancelled on disappear.
Why it matters
The address line under the map can show the address of an earlier pin position. Today save() does its own lookup, so this only affects the display. If save is changed to persist geocodedAddress (recommended above), the stale value would be stored.
How to fix
After the await, add `guard !Task.isCancelled else { return }` before writing geocodedAddress or isGeocoding. Alternatively, capture the coordinate and compare it with selectedCoordinate before assigning. Cancel geocodeTask in onDisappear.
Expected improvement
The displayed address always matches the current pin.
Evidence
geocodeTask = Task {
    try? await Task.sleep(nanoseconds: 800_000_000)
    guard !Task.isCancelled else { return }
    isGeocoding = true
    geocodedAddress = await reverseGeocode(coordinate) ?? geocodedAddress
    isGeocoding = false
}
Verifier notes (2)
  • CONFIRM (existence lens, high confidence): I read /Users/daniel/Projects/pitstop/Pitstop/Pitstop/AddBathroomView.swift, lines 741-755 (scheduleGeocode), 772-789 (reverseGeocode and candidateAddress) and 796-801 (save). The finding is accurate. - **Cancellation check:** scheduleGeocode cancels geocodeTask, then starts a Task that sleeps 800 ms and checks Task.isCancelled once. After `await reverseGeocode(coordinate)` returns, it writes geocodedAddress and sets isGeocoding = false with no second check. - **reverseGeocode ignores cancellation:** it uses `try? await request.mapItems`. A cancelled MKReverseGeocodingRequest throws, so the call returns nil and the `?? geocodedAddress` fallback keeps the current value. But if the request completes before cancellation takes effect, the old coordinate's address is written. - **isGeocoding:** the cancelled task always sets isGeocoding = false, even while a newer task is still pending. - **Candidate path:** the candidateAddress early return (lines 744-746) cancels the old task and sets the Candidate's address. An in-flight old lookup can still overwrite it afterwards. - **onDisappear:** the file has no onDisappear modifier. geocodeTask is cancelled only in scheduleGeocode and in save() (line 798). Corrections: - **Line numbers:** the cited 742-755 is right. The function signature is at 742 and the Task body is at 748-754. - **Impact:** smaller than "never cancelled on disappear" suggests. A leftover task only writes @State on a view that is already gone, so the only visible effect is the stale address line while the view is open. - **Severity:** low is right, since save() does its own lookup. Fix: the suggested `guard !Task.isCancelled` after the await works. Comparing the captured coordinate with selectedCoordinate is more robust. Only set isGeocoding = false when the task was not cancelled.
  • CONFIRM (impact lens, medium confidence): I read Pitstop/Pitstop/AddBathroomView.swift: scheduleGeocode at lines 742-755, the onChange triggers at 174-175, onAppear at 304-306, reverseGeocode at 772-779, candidateAddress at 783-788 and save() starting at 796. The code matches the evidence. After the sleep there is only one cancellation check, and nothing checks again after `await reverseGeocode(coordinate)` before writing geocodedAddress and isGeocoding. Impact is smaller than the description suggests: (1) In the usual case (the pin moves to another spot that is not on the Candidate), the stale write is brief. The new task sleeps 800 ms, runs its own lookup and overwrites the value. A stale value only lasts if the new lookup returns nil, because `?? geocodedAddress` keeps whatever is there. (2) The lasting case is the candidateAddress early return. If you drag the pin off the Candidate, a lookup starts, and you drag back within 10 m, no new task is created. The old lookup can then overwrite the Candidate's address, and it stays that way. (3) It is not certain the stale write happens at all. If MKReverseGeocodingRequest.mapItems honors task cancellation and throws, `try?` turns that into nil and geocodedAddress is left alone. Only `isGeocoding = false` would be wrong, and only briefly. (4) save() cancels geocodeTask and does its own lookup through candidateAddress/reverseGeocode, so nothing stale is saved today. Display only, as the finding says. (5) Not cancelling on disappear does no harm in practice. The late write goes to the @State of a view that is already gone. On the fix: a `guard !Task.isCancelled` after the await, or comparing the captured coordinate with selectedCoordinate, is correct and small. Severity low is right, and info would also be defensible.

Pitstop/Pitstop/AddBathroomView.swift:159-162 · reviewer: add-edit-form

Pass the location manager (or a binding) instead of a snapshot. While in add mode, if the user has not moved the pin yet, set mapJump to the first non-nil location when it arrives. Use a VA/DC default instead of NYC.

Sheet identity is the persistentModelID, which changes when a new Bathroom is first saved

Pitstop/Pitstop/MapTabView.swift:106-115 · reviewer: detail-views-ui-state
What it is
`MapSelection.id` returns `persistentModelID`, and both tabs present `PlaceDetailSheet` with `.sheet(item:)` keyed on it. A newly inserted Bathroom has a temporary persistent identifier until the context is saved. The app never saves explicitly (AddBathroomView.swift:835 only inserts), so the identifier changes from temporary to permanent whenever autosave runs. If the user opens the detail sheet for a bathroom they just logged and autosave runs while it is open, `sheet(item:)` sees a new identity and can dismiss and re-present the sheet. `ForEach(places)` in ListTabView uses the same id, so it can also rebuild the row.
Why it matters
A sheet that closes and reopens right after logging a visit looks broken, and any state inside it (an open edit cover, the delete alert) is lost.
How to fix
Key the selection on a stable value: `bathroom.id` (the model's UUID) for bathrooms and `candidate.sourceID` for Candidates, wrapped in an enum or string id. Alternatively, save explicitly right after insert so the identifier is permanent before the pin can be tapped.
Expected improvement
The sheet's and the rows' identity stay stable across saves.
Evidence
var id: PersistentIdentifier {
    switch self {
    case .bathroom(let bathroom):   return bathroom.persistentModelID
    case .candidate(let candidate): return candidate.persistentModelID
Verifier notes (2)

A new Bathroom therefore keeps its temporary SwiftData identifier until autosave runs, and that identifier changes when the model is saved. This is documented SwiftData behavior. Because `id` is computed live from the model, the identity of the selection held in state really can change while a sheet is open. Corrections: the enum spans lines 105-116, so the cited 106-115 is close enough. I did not run the app, so I have not confirmed the dismiss-and-re-present symptom on a device. In practice the window is narrow. Autosave usually fires soon after the insert, and the user has to tap the new pin or row inside that window. Low severity fits. EmergencyButton checks Reduce Motion only once, on appear

Pitstop/Pitstop/EmergencyButton.swift:38-46 · reviewer: detail-views-ui-state
What it is
The repeating shadow pulse starts in `onAppear` if Reduce Motion is off and is never stopped. The button lives on the long-lived map screen, so turning Reduce Motion on while the app is running leaves it pulsing forever. Nothing reacts to `reduceMotion` changing. The pulse also keeps running while `isWorking` shows the spinner.
Why it matters
The README promises animations that respect Reduce Motion. Users who turn it on mid-session, often because motion bothers them, still get a perpetual animation on the most prominent control.
How to fix
Add `.onChange(of: reduceMotion)` that sets `isPulsing = false` inside a transaction with no animation when Reduce Motion turns on, and restarts the repeating animation when it turns off. Or drive the radius from `reduceMotion ? 6 : (isPulsing ? 12 : 6)` so the setting wins at render time.
Expected improvement
The pulse stops as soon as the user enables Reduce Motion, matching the README.
Evidence
.onAppear {
    guard !reduceMotion else { return }
    withAnimation(
        .easeInOut(duration: 1.5)
        .repeatForever(autoreverses: true)
    ) {
        isPulsing = true
Verifier notes (2)

There is no `.onChange(of: reduceMotion)`, and the shadow radius at line 33 (`isPulsing ? 12 : 6`) does not check `reduceMotion` when it renders. The pulse also keeps running while `isWorking` shows the ProgressView, so that part of the claim is accurate too. In ContentView.swift (lines 68-78) the EmergencyButton sits in the root ZStack outside the Map/List `switch`. It is always on screen on both tabs, so it is even more long-lived than the finding says, and `onAppear` will rarely fire again to re-check the setting. The finding has the direction right. When Reduce Motion is turned on mid-session, the pulse keeps going. When it is turned off mid-session, the pulse never starts, which is harmless. The second suggested fix is the simpler one: `radius: (reduceMotion || !isPulsing) ? 6 : 12`. A repeatForever animation that is already running may still blend in until the value actually changes, so pairing this with an `onChange` that resets `isPulsing` inside a transaction with animations disabled is the most reliable approach. Seed version resets to 1 when the output file does not exist, which the app silently ignores

scripts/seed/build_seed.py:367-373 · reviewer: seed-pipeline-python
What it is
`next_version` derives the version only from whatever file is at `--output`. Writing to a fresh path (to stage a seed for review before copying it into the app, or after the bundled file was deleted or moved) returns version 1. On devices the last imported version is already 4, and `importIfNewer` returns false when `seed.version > defaults.integer(...)` fails, with no log.
Why it matters
A seed staged this way and copied into the app would never be imported. Users would keep stale Candidates and nothing would say why.
How to fix
Always base the version on the bundled seed (DEFAULT_OUTPUT) when it exists, even if `--output` points elsewhere, or add a `--base-version` argument. Alternatively, exit with an error when the output is missing unless `--first-version` is passed.
Expected improvement
Every generated seed has a version above the one already shipped, so the app imports it.
Evidence
if not output.exists():
    return 1
previous = json.loads(output.read_text())

The simplest fix is to base the version on DEFAULT_OUTPUT when `--output` differs, or to refuse a missing output without an explicit flag. Logging when `importIfNewer` skips would also make the problem visible on device.

Overlapping PhotosPicker load tasks can push the photo count past 10

Pitstop/Pitstop/AddBathroomView.swift:726-738 · reviewer: concurrency-swiftdata
What it is
`loadPickerItems` starts a new unstructured `Task` on each `photoPickerItems` change, never cancels an earlier one, and checks `totalPhotoCount < 10` before awaiting `loadTransferable`, then appends after the await. If a second picker selection arrives while the first task is still loading (the picker is re-enabled because `totalPhotoCount` has not been updated yet), both tasks can pass the check at the same count and both append. Each task also clears `photoPickerItems = []` when it finishes, even if the other task is still working through its items.
Why it matters
The 10-photo cap shown as `\(totalPhotoCount)/10` can be exceeded, and every extra photo means more main-thread encode work during Save. It is minor, but it comes from unstructured tasks that are never tracked.
How to fix
Keep the load task in `@State`, cancel it before starting a new one, and re-check `totalPhotoCount < 10` right before `selectedPhotos.append(image)`.
Expected improvement
The photo cap holds, and picker loads cannot interleave.
Evidence
Task {
    for item in items {
        guard totalPhotoCount < 10 else { break }
        if let data  = try? await item.loadTransferable(type: Data.self),
           let image = UIImage(data: data) {
            selectedPhotos.append(image)
        }
    }
    photoPickerItems = []
}
Verifier notes (2)
  • CONFIRM (existence lens, medium confidence): I read /Users/daniel/Projects/pitstop/Pitstop/Pitstop/AddBathroomView.swift. The cited code is real and matches the evidence exactly. `loadPickerItems` (lines 726-738) is called from `.onChange(of: photoPickerItems)` (lines 307-309). It starts an unstructured `Task` that is never stored or cancelled. Inside it, `guard totalPhotoCount < 10` runs before `await item.loadTransferable`, and `selectedPhotos.append(image)` runs after the await without checking the count again. When the task finishes it sets `photoPickerItems = []`. While a load is in flight, `totalPhotoCount` (line 87, existing plus selected) has not gone up yet. So the Library picker (lines 615-622) stays enabled, and its `maxSelectionCount: max(0, 10 - totalPhotoCount)` is computed from that stale count. A second selection starts a second task. The two tasks interleave on the main actor at their await points, so both can pass the guard at 9 and both append, which takes the count past 10. A second path the finding does not mention: the Camera button (line 627) is also enabled during the load. Its handler (line 299) checks `totalPhotoCount < 10` at that moment, so a camera photo plus an in-flight library load can also exceed the cap. The fix (re-check the count right before the append) covers both paths. One small overstatement: the "clears photoPickerItems while the other task is still working" part does no harm. Each task iterates its own captured `items` array, and setting the selection to [] only triggers `onChange` with an empty list, which returns early. A side effect of not clearing until the end: reopening the picker mid-load may show the earlier items still preselected, so a second selection can load them again as duplicates. The trigger needs the user to reopen the picker before the first load finishes, which is a narrow window for local photos, so low severity is right. This is not one of the excluded known fixes.
  • CONFIRM (impact lens, medium confidence): I read Pitstop/Pitstop/AddBathroomView.swift: loadPickerItems (lines 726-738), the onChange(of: photoPickerItems) hookup (307-309), the PhotosPicker and its maxSelectionCount/disabled guards (611-627), the camera append (299), and the selectedPhotos uses up to Save (812, 830). The code matches the evidence. Each onChange starts an untracked Task (MainActor, inherited from the View). That Task checks `totalPhotoCount < 10` before `await loadTransferable` and appends after it, with no re-check and no cancellation. Save does not enforce the cap either; it loops over every selectedPhotos entry. So nothing elsewhere guards against this. Corrections and nuance: (1) The overshoot is small. Everything runs on the main actor, so each concurrent task can have at most one item past the check while it awaits. With two overlapping tasks the count can reach about 11, not grow without limit. (2) The bigger effect of the overlap is probably duplicate photos, which the finding misses. Until the first task finishes, photoPickerItems still holds the first selection. Reopening the picker shows those items preselected, so the next selection is usually a superset, and the second task loads the first batch again. (3) The window is narrow. Local library images load almost instantly. Only slow loads, such as originals downloaded from iCloud, leave time to reopen the picker. That keeps severity at low. (4) The proposed fix is right, but its two parts do different jobs. Re-checking `totalPhotoCount < 10` just before `selectedPhotos.append` is enough to fix the cap. Storing the Task in @State and cancelling it on each new selection (or skipping items already loaded) is what prevents the duplicate reloads. This does not duplicate any of the excluded or recently fixed items.
